► Prise en compte de l’incertitude et analyse de sensibilité
Analyse de sensibilité déterministe
Une analyse de sensibilité déterministe a été réalisée sur certains paramètres, variant générale-ment de +/-25%, dont les résultats sont présentés pour les paramètres les plus sensibles.
Analyse de sensibilité probabiliste
Une analyse de sensibilité probabiliste a été réalisée, portant sur les valeurs d’utilité, les coûts, l’évolution de la maladie et l’efficacité du traitement.
Analyse des limites et de la validité de l’étude
Les limites de l’étude ne sont pas présentées.
3.6.2 Analyse critique de la présentation des résultats et de l’analyse de sensibilité
► Résultats de l’étude économique
Le RDCR dans l’ensemble des populations atteintes d’hépatite C chronique ne peut pas être rete-nu compte tenu de la forte hétérogénéité des résultats entre les sous-populations et de l’existence de comparateurs multiples pour certaines sous-populations.
La présentation des résultats par sous-population est confuse dans la mesure où le comparateur n’est pas systématiquement rappelé. En particulier, les situations de dominance dans le cas où plusieurs comparateurs sont intégrés dans l’analyse ne semblent pas avoir été analysées.
Les résultats des analyses complémentaires n’ont pas été fournis.
► Prise en compte de l’incertitude et analyse de sensibilité
Analyse de sensibilité déterministe
Les paramètres testés ne sont pas tous présentés, et la variation de la valeur des paramètres (+/- 25%) n’est pas argumentée.
Les graphiques de Tornado concernant les populations du génotype 1 transmis lors de l’échange technique semblent erronés (valeurs du RDCR différentes des valeurs transmises dans le ta-bleau).
Analyse de sensibilité probabiliste
L’analyse de sensibilité probabiliste est correctement décrite. La variation des paramètres d’efficacité des traitements soulève néanmoins des questions dans la mesure où la source des variations n’est pas décrite, et conduit, dans certaines sous-populations à envisager des taux d’efficacité supérieurs à 100%, et atteignant 120%.

Sovaldi® (sofosbuvir) – Avis d’efficience
HAS / Service évaluation économique et santé publique 24
Analyse des limites et de la validité de l’étude
L’absence de discussion des limites de l’étude, en particulier sur la validité des données d’efficacité est une limite importante à l’étude réalisée. Concernant la validité des données cli-niques, l’impact de la nature non comparative de certaines données n’est pas discuté. De plus, l’intégration de l’incertitude relative à ces données dans l’analyse de sensibilité probabiliste n’est pas correcte.
3.7 Remarques générales
Le dossier est d’une façon générale peu argumenté. De nombreux éléments manquants dans le dossier initial ont dû être demandés au cours de l’échange technique (cf. liste de questions). Les questions posées lors de l’échange technique n’ont porté que sur les éléments jugés les plus im-portants pour l’analyse critique de l’étude. Pour cette raison, certains éléments considérés comme moins essentiels restent non documentés.
Des réponses ont été apportées aux questions techniques. Néanmoins, ces réponses sont par-tielles, par la présentation d’un nombre limité de résultats (absence d’analyse sur les données en vie réelle, absence de détails sur la mise à jour des coûts, absence de résultats chiffrés de l’impact de la variation du prix du sofosbuvir sur le RDCR dans la nouvelle modélisation). Certaines don-nées et certains résultats semblent erronés (graphiques de Tornado pour les sous-populations du génotype 1 dont la valeur de référence est différente du résultat présenté par ailleurs, taux d’efficacité atteignant 120% dans l’analyse de sensibilité probabiliste). La transmission sous format électronique du modèle mis à jour aurait été souhaitable. Enfin, des données complémentaires demandées n’ont pas été transmises (annexe sur l’étude de coût, données complémentaires sur les critères d’efficacité).

HAS / Service évaluation économique et santé publique 25
4.
Annexe 4
–
Synthèse de l’analyse critique
Les éléments de critique identifiés dans l’analyse détaillée sont hiérarchisés selon trois niveaux.
 Réserve mineure (-) : élément non conforme aux recommandations en vigueur, mais qui est justifié ou dont l’impact attendu sur les conclusions est négligeable.
 Réserve importante (+) : élément non conforme aux recommandations en vigueur, avec un impact attendu important sur les conclusions (en particulier en termes d’incertitude).
 Réserve majeure (++) : élément non conforme aux recommandations en vigueur qui invalide tout ou partie de l’étude économique.

5.
Annexe 5
–
Echange avec
l’industriel
La liste de questions techniques ci-dessous a été adressée à l’industriel. Seules les questions por-tant sur des éléments jugés susceptibles de modifier de façon significative le résultat obtenu sont posées aux auteurs de l’étude.
L’industriel a adressé des réponses écrites à la HAS.
Les éléments en gras doivent être traités en priorité.
Concernant la modélisation réalisée
Le modèle ne distingue pas les stades de fibrose. Vous citez comme référence le modèle de Bennett et al. (1997) à l’appui de ce choix méthodologique, tout en mentionnant l’existence de modèles plus récents distinguant les stades de fibrose (Deuffic-Durban et al., 2008 par exemple).
1. Les recommandations actuelles de traitement dépendant notamment du stade de fi-brose, il est attendu un modèle distinguant les différents stades de fibrose ou à tout le moins, précisant à quel stade de fibrose les patients sont traités, pour le produit évalué et ses comparateurs. Le cas échéant, une comparaison des stratégies en fonction du stade de fibrose auquel les patients sont traités peut être envisagée.
Concernant la population simulée dans le modèle
2. La population des patients éligibles à la greffe ou en post-greffe (population pour laquelle le produit a une ATU de cohorte) n’est pas simulée dans le modèle. Quels sont les résultats de l’évaluation économique pour cette population ?
Les sous-groupes suivants ne sont pas considérés :
 GT 1 expérimenté
 GT 3 naïf co-infecté VIH
 GT 4/5/6 expérimentés
 GT 4/5/6 inéligibles à l’IFN
3. Quelle est l’importance de ces 4 sous-populations dans la population française des patients avec une hépatite C chronique ?
Concernant les stratégies comparées
Dans l’analyse principale, pour le génotype 1 co-infecté VHC/VIH, le comparateur est l’association PEG + RBV. Or, les recommandations françaises et européennes (cf. page 14 du rapport technique) préconisent une trithérapie avec inhibiteur de protéase.
4. Quelle est la justification de ce choix méthodologique ?
Concernant les hypothèses simplificatrices
Des hypothèses simplificatrices ont été retenues dans le modèle. Une validation argumentée de ces hypothèses est attendue lorsque ces choix sont susceptibles d’être favorables au sofosbuvir dans l’évaluation.
5. Concernant le choix de ne pas faire évoluer dans le modèle les patients cirrhotiques avec RVS (hors décès autres causes), quel est l’impact de ce choix sur les résultats de l’évaluation ? Cet impact peut être estimé par une documentation plus précise de l’évolution de la maladie (risque d’évolution de la cirrhose, risque de mortalité spécifique, risque de ré-infection, délai de retour à un niveau de risque similaire à la population non-infectée).

Sovaldi® (sofosbuvir) – Avis d’efficience
HAS / Service évaluation économique et santé publique 27
Concernant les valeurs d’utilité retenues
Chez les mono-infectés, le score est plus élevé pour la cirrhose décompensée (0,56) que pour la cirrhose compensée (0,51) (page 37 du rapport technique).
6. Comment expliquez-vous ces résultats contre-intuitifs ?
Concernant les coûts de suivi
Une validation argumentée des choix méthodologiques est attendue lorsque ces choix sont susceptibles d’être favorables au sofosbuvir dans l’évaluation. En l’absence de justification, une analyse moins favorable au traitement évalué doit être réalisée.
7. Concernant les coûts de suivi, comment justifiez-vous :
a. L’application de coûts de suivi (« monitoring ») lorsque le comparateur est l’absence de traitement ?
b. L’écart du nombre de visites entre le sofosbuvir et le comparateur (par exemple pour 24 semaines de traitement chez un patient non cirrhotique : 10 visites pour le comparateur (S0, S1, S2, S4, S6, S8, S12, S16, S20 et S24) versus 7 visites pour sofosbuvir (S0, S1, S4, S8, S12, S16 et S24) ?
c. L’absence de visites de suivi supplémentaires chez un patient cirrhotique traité par sofosbuvir alors que ces visites sont prévues pour les comparateurs (tableau 35 page 86 du rapport technique : suivi à S10, S14, S18 et S22) ?
d. L’absence d’examen radiologique à S24 et la fibroscopie à S48 chez les patients cir-rhotiques traités par sofosbuvir alors que ces coûts sont intégrés pour les traite-ments comparateurs ?
Des données n’ont pas été fournies dans le rapport d’évaluation et sont attendues :
 Les résultats moyens par patient pour chacun des sous-groupes considérés (cor-respondant aux résultats présentés dans les tableaux 20, 21 et 22 du rapport tech-nique)
 Les analyses de sensibilité déterministes et probabilistes pour chacun des sous-groupes considérés (en argumentant le choix des paramètres testés et leur variation)
 Les résultats des analyses complémentaires
 Les annexes mentionnées pages 33 (revue systématique de la littérature sur les études) et 66 (revue de la littérature sur la distribution des paramètres)
 Les « données de la littérature » permettant d’estimer les taux d’évènements indésirables des comparateurs (page 43 du rapport technique)
 Une copie des références PC Hsu, 2012 et Mcgreal-Bellone et al. 2012
 Les rapports des études de micro-costing réalisées
 Le tableau sur la variation du paramètre « taux de réponse prolongée » dans l’analyse de sensibilité probabiliste
Points divers
 A quoi correspondent les flèches grises en pointillés dans la description de la structure du modèle (figure 5 page 22 du rapport technique) ?

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division

1/39

Actual Benefit

Given:

Improvement in
Actual Benefit

Therapeutic Use

Recommendations

-

-

-

its higher efficacy for the most difficult to treat patients, in particular genotype 1, 4, 5
and  6  patients,  cirrhotic  patients,  pre-transplantation  patients  and  patients
co-infected with HIV and HCV,
its  efficacy  for  genotype  2  and  3  patients,  including  those  with  a  failed  previous
treatment, but with a higher response for genotype 2 patients (a short-term 12 week
treatment  of  dual  therapy  with  ribavirin,  without  interferon)  than  for  genotype
3 patients  (requiring  extension  of  treatment  to  24  weeks  with  dual  therapy  with
ribavirin, or even short-term 12 week treatment as triple therapy with interferon),
its  satisfactory  safety,  resistance  and  interaction  with  other  medicines  profiles
observed during clinical studies,

the  Committee  considers  that  SOVALDI,  in  combination  with  pegylated  interferon  alpha
and/or ribavirin, provides:

-

-

a  substantial  improvement  in  actual  benefit  (IAB  II)  in  the  treatment  of  all  adult
patients infected with HCV, except for antiviral treatment-naïve genotype 3 patients.
a  moderate  improvement  in  actual  benefit  (IAB  III)  in  the  treatment  of  antiviral
treatment-naïve adult patients infected with genotype 3 HCV.

Given  the  levels  of  virological  efficacy  irrespective  of  HCV  genotype  or  severity  of
hepatic  impairment  and  its  satisfactory  safety  profile,  sofosbuvir,  in  combination  with
other antivirals, represents the new standard treatment for patients with chronic hepatitis
C.
According  to  the  DHUMEAUX1  report,  it  is  recommended  to  propose  treatment  with
sofosbuvir  as  a  priority  for  all  patients  with  a  fibrosis  score  ≥  F2  as  well  as  for  certain
special  populations,  independent  of  the  degree  of  fibrosis:  “patients  with  extrahepatic
manifestations  of  the  hepatitis  C  virus,  patients  awaiting  an  organ  transplant,  women
wishing to become pregnant, drug users and those in prison.”

SOVALDI  is  the  first  new  substance,  which  when  combined  with  other  antivirals,
whether  they  are  specific  or  not,  could  be  able  to  cure  the  majority  of  patients  with
hepatitis C, with or without extrahepatic manifestations. Treatment strategies are being
completely renewed and new substances, which may be prescribed in combination with
sofosbuvir or other substances, are expected in the coming months.

The  Committee  would  therefore  like  to  re-assess  this  medicinal  product  in  the
short-term,  depending  on  the  developments  in  the  clinical  data  and  the  scope  of  the
treatment of chronic hepatitis C.

According  to  the  DHUMEAUX  report,  it  is  already  desirable  to  propose  treatment  with
sofosbuvir  as  a  priority  for  all  patients  whose  liver  condition  is  at  a  F3  or  F4  stage  of
fibrosis. Patients at the F2 stage of fibrosis should also be given the new treatments as
soon as possible. For F0 and F1 patients, based on disease progression, which is often
very  slow  over  several  years  allowing  the  physician  to  suspend  the  prescription  while
waiting for clarification of therapeutic use, the treatment may be deferred.

It is also recommended to treat certain special populations, independent of the degree
of fibrosis, such as patients waiting for an organ transplant, women wishing to become
pregnant, drug users, patients co-infected with HIV and those who are in prison as well
as patients who are presenting with extrahepatic manifestations of the hepatitis C virus.

Extension  of  the  treatment  to  the  entire  population  infected  with  HCV  can  only  be
proposed through an overall public health approach.

(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) Specific requests inherent to reimbursement

The Committee recommends that this medicinal product is given exception drug status

02  BACKGROUND

Sofosbuvir  (SOVALDI)  is  an  antiviral  medicinal  product,  active  against  the  hepatitis  C  virus  (HCV),
coming from a new therapeutic category (hepatitis C specific nucleotide analogue, NS5B polymerase
inhibitor), with a direct pan-genotypic action (active against all HCV genotypes: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6).

Sofosbuvir is administered orally, taken once daily, in combination with ribavirin, on its own in cases
of  genotype  2  or  3  virus  or  in  combination  with  pegylated  interferon  (PEG)  +  ribavirin  (RBV)  for
genotype 1, 3, 4, 5 or 6 virus.

This  is  a  hepatitis  C  treatment,  enabling  in  some  patients  treatment  without  interferon  and  with
reduced  treatment  duration  (12  to  24  weeks  versus  24  to  48  weeks  with  current  treatments). It  has
few interactions with other medicines, and in particular none with treatments against HIV infection, or
with  immunosuppressant  treatments,  which  are  a  major  benefit  for  transplant  patients.  There  is  no
interaction with methadone, which is important for addicts taking this medicinal product.

Sofosbuvir has had temporary authorisation for use by a cohort [ATU de cohorte] since 30 September
2013, for adult patients with chronic hepatitis C infection:
-

presenting with an advanced stage of the disease for which there are no appropriate
alternative treatments,

on a waiting list for a liver transplant,

or
-
or
-  who have undergone a liver transplant and are presenting with an aggressive recurrence of

the hepatitis C infection.

The main information relating to the temporary authorisation for use by a cohort [ATU de cohorte] (to
07/01/2014) is presented in Section 8.3.

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

3/37

03  THERAPEUTIC INDICATION

“SOVALDI is indicated in combination with other medicinal products for the treatment of chronic
hepatitis C (CHC) in adults (see sections 4.2, 4.4 and 5.1 of the SPC).

For hepatitis C virus (HCV) genotype specific activity, see sections 4.4 and 5.1 of the SPC.”

04  DOSAGE

“SOVALDI  treatment  should  be  initiated  and  monitored  by  a  physician  experienced  in  the
management of patients with CHC.

Posology
The recommended dose is one 400 mg tablet, taken orally, once daily with food (see section 5.2 of
the SPC).

SOVALDI should be used in combination with other medicinal products. Monotherapy of SOVALDI is
not recommended (see section 5.1 of the SPC). Refer also to the Summary of Product Characteristics
of  the  medicinal  products  that  are  used  in  combination  with  SOVALDI.  The  recommended
co-administered  medicinal  product(s)  and  treatment  duration  for  SOVALDI  combination  therapy  are
provided in Table 1.

Opinion 1

4/37

The dose of ribavirin, when used in combination with SOVALDI is weight-based (<75 kg = 1000 mg
and ≥75 kg = 1200 mg) and administered orally in two divided doses with food.

Concerning co-administration with other direct-acting antivirals against HCV, see section 4.4 of the
SPC.

Dose modification
Dose reduction of SOVALDI is not recommended.

If  sofosbuvir  is  used  in  combination  with  peginterferon  alpha,  and  a  patient  has  a  serious  adverse
reaction  potentially  related  to  this  drug,  the  peginterferon  alpha  dose  should  be  reduced  or
discontinued.  Refer  to  the  peginterferon  alpha  Summary  of  Product  Characteristics  for  additional
information about how to reduce and/or discontinue the peginterferon alpha dose.

If a patient has a serious adverse reaction potentially related to ribavirin, the ribavirin dose should be
modified or discontinued, if appropriate, until the adverse reaction abates or decreases in severity.
Table  2  provides  guidelines  for  dose  modifications  and  discontinuation  based  on  the  patient's
haemoglobin concentration and cardiac status.

Table 2: Ribavirin dose modification guideline for co-administration with SOVALDI

cardiac disease

of stable cardiac disease

reduced dose

Once ribavirin has been withheld due to either a laboratory abnormality or clinical manifestation, an
attempt may be made to restart ribavirin at 600 mg daily and further increase the dose to 800 mg
daily. However, it is not recommended that ribavirin be increased to the original assigned dose
(1000 mg to 1200 mg daily).

Discontinuation of dosing
If  the  other  medicinal  products  used  in  combination  with  SOVALDI  are  permanently  discontinued,
SOVALDI should also be discontinued (see section 4.4 of the SPC).

Special patient populations
Elderly
No dose adjustment is warranted for elderly patients (see section 5.2 of the SPC).

Renal impairment
No  dose  adjustment  of  SOVALDI  is  required  for  patients  with  mild  or  moderate  renal  impairment.
The  safety  and  appropriate  dose  of  SOVALDI  have  not  been  established  in  patients  with  severe
renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] <30 ml/min/1.73 m2) or end stage renal
disease (ESRD) requiring haemodialysis (see section 5.2 of the SPC).

Hepatic impairment
No  dose  adjustment  of  SOVALDI  is  required  for  patients  with  mild,  moderate  or  severe  hepatic
impairment (Child-Pugh-Turcotte [CPT] class A, B or C) (see section 5.2 of the SPC). The safety and
efficacy of SOVALDI have not been established in patients with decompensated cirrhosis.

Patients awaiting liver transplantation
The duration of administration of SOVALDI in patients awaiting liver transplantation should be guided
by an assessment of the potential benefits and risks for the individual patient (see section 5.1 of the

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

5/37

SPC).

Paediatric population
The safety and efficacy of SOVALDI in children and adolescents aged <18 years have not yet been
established. No data are available.

Method of administration
The film-coated tablet is for oral use. Patients should be instructed to swallow the tablet whole. The
film-coated tablet should not be chewed or crushed, due to the bitter taste of the active substance.
The tablet should be taken with food (see section 5.2 of the SPC).

Patients  should  be  instructed  that  if  vomiting  occurs  within  2  hours  of  dosing  an  additional  tablet
should be taken. If vomiting occurs more than 2 hours after dosing, no further dose is needed. These
recommendations  are  based  on  the  absorption  kinetics  of  sofosbuvir  and  GS-331007  suggesting
that the majority of the dose is absorbed within 2 hours after dosing.

If a dose is missed and it is within 18 hours of the normal time, patients should be instructed to take
the tablet as soon as possible and then patients should take the next dose at the usual time. If it is
after 18 hours then patients should be instructed to wait and take the next dose at the usual time.
Patients should be instructed not to take a double dose.”

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

6/37

05  THERAPEUTIC NEED2,3,4,5,6,7

The severity of hepatitis C is linked to it commonly becoming a chronic condition, which in the
long-term can lead to cirrhosis (in 10 to 20% of cases), after a median of 10 to 30 years, depending
on whether or not there are aggravating co-factors present (male, alcohol consumption, HIV
co-infection and the level of immunodepression and HIV replication, steatosis-hepatitis, age at the
time of contraction etc.) or even hepatocellular carcinoma (1 to 5% of cirrhosis cases per year).

HCV has six different genotypes, and patients are generally infected by a single genotype. In France,
genotype 1 (1a and in particular 1b) is the most common (61.1%), followed by genotype 3 (18.6%);
genotypes  2  (8.7%),  4  (9.1%),  5  (1.9%)  and  6  (0.6%)  are  more  rare.  Genotypes  1  and  4  are
associated with a poorer response to treatment, while genotype 3 has a higher risk of liver steatosis.
During diagnosis, the initial assessment specifically includes virological analysis (HCV genotyping)
and an assessment to investigate the signs of severity (clinical, laboratory, morphological and
radiological assessment). The HCV genotype influences both the therapeutic management and the
response to treatment. Liver histology enables differentiation between barely active and active chronic
hepatitis and a histological cirrhosis diagnosis to be made. The METAVIR8 score assesses
inflammatory activity (A0 to A3) and fibrosis (F0 to F4) separately.
The  aim  of  treatment  is  to  cure  the  infection,  defined  by  the  sustained  virological  response  (SVR)
i.e. an undetectable viral load (HCV RNA) 24 weeks (or 12 weeks) after the end of treatment. As an
indication, hepatitis C treatment is proposed in the following circumstances:

-

-

-
-
-

In cases of HCV genotype indicative of a good therapeutic response (2 or 3) regardless of
the level of liver fibrosis;
In cases of genotype 1 or 4, when there is septal (F ≥ 2) or portal (F1) fibrosis combined
with significant signs of activity (A2 or A3);
In cases of acute hepatitis;
In cases of compensated cirrhosis;
In cases of HIV-HCV co-infection with the same indications as for patients who are
mono-infected;
In cases of severe extrahepatic manifestations, in particular cryoglobulinaemia;

-
-  When there is a need for viral eradication, in particular at the request of the patient or within

the context of fertility treatment or pregnancy;

-  For transplant patients. It should be noted that HCV relapse is practically constant for

patients, and that HCV treatment can be proposed in this context.

Treatment of chronic hepatitis C is currently based on the use of interferon and ribavirin, prescribed
jointly,  in  association  or  not  with  protease  inhibitors  (boceprevir  and  telaprevir  for  genotype
1 patients). Currently, there is no strategy without the use of interferon.

Up  to  2011,  pegylated  interferon  alpha/ribavirin  (PEG-INF/RBV)  dual  therapy  over  24  to  48  weeks
was  the  standard  treatment  for  hepatitis  C.  On  average,  this  treatment  results  in  an  SVR  of  65%,

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

7/37

depending  on  the  viral  genotype  (genotype  1  patients  being  the  most  difficult  to  treat:  SVR  of
approximately 50% versus 80 to 85% for genotypes 2-3). Factors associated with a poor response to
dual therapy are high viral load, genotypes 1 or 4, and co-infection with HIV. It should be noted that
interferon is poorly tolerated (flu-like syndrome, depression, etc.) and safety decreases with age and
in cases of advanced fibrosis. Intolerance results in discontinuation of treatment in 10 to 30% of cases
within  the  first  6  months.  The  main  adverse  effect  of  ribavirin,  which  is  more  or  less  constant,  is  a
lowering of haemoglobin levels (by approximately 13% during the first 2 months), due to haemolysis.
For  patients  infected  with  genotype  1  HCV,  the  arrival  of  new,  direct  action  substances  (HCV
protease  inhibitors:  boceprevir  and  telaprevir)9
,10  has  seen  changes  made  to  the  treatment  strategy
since  2011.  In  this  population,  triple  therapy  (peginterferon  +  ribavirin  +  protease  inhibitor)  is  a
treatment  method  appropriate  for  some  patient  profiles,  who  have  not  previously  been  treated  (in
particular patients who do not have factors indicative of a good response to peginterferon + ribavirin
dual
therapy
(peginterferon + ribavirin). Triple therapy (peginterferon + ribavirin + protease inhibitor) increases the
efficacy of genotype 1 hepatitis C antiviral treatment, enabling a cure (SVR) for 65 to 75% of patients,
sometimes  with  a  24  week  treatment  course  (particularly  for  non-cirrhotic  patients,  not  previously
treated  or  relapsing  patients  achieving  a  fast  response  during  treatment).  Even  in  the  presence  of
factors  indicative  of  a  poor  response  to  treatment  (IL28B  non-CC  genotype,  F3-F4  fibrosis),  the
chance of a cure with triple therapy remains high and greater than 50%, with a significant benefit for
patients  compared  with  dual  therapy.  Nevertheless,  the  adverse  effects  linked  to  interferon  and
ribavirin are increased within the context of this triple therapy (in particular, increased haematological
toxicity),  with  more  frequent  treatment  discontinuation.  The  management  of  adverse  effects  and
potential interactions with other medicines requires close monitoring of the patient by the doctor and
others involved in the care pathway.

for  some  patients  with

therapy)  and

It should be noted that interferon alpha is contraindicated in the following circumstances:

-  auto-immune hepatitis,
-
transplant patients treated with immunosuppressants,
-  psychiatric disorders or decompensated thyroid conditions,
severe renal impairment,
-
-  decompensated cirrhosis.

Given  these  limitations,  there  is  a  therapeutic  need  for  medicines  to  be  available  that  are  at
least  as  effective  as  those  involved  in  current  therapeutic  strategies  (based  on  the  use  of
interferon and ribavirin), with a better safety and resistance profile, enabling the development
of interferon-free strategies (or even ribavirin-free) and to expand the coverage to patients with
a higher medical need (particularly non-eligible patients or non-responders to interferon).

06.1  Medicinal products

Current treatment for hepatitis C, independent of the genotype involved, is based on the combination
of pegylated interferon + ribavirin (PEG-INF + RBV).

Since 2011, for patients with a genotype 1 virus, treatments from the protease inhibitors category
(boceprevir and telaprevir) have been used with PEG-INF+RBV.

06.2  Other information

Currently, two medicinal products have a temporary authorisation for use by a cohort [ATU de
cohorte] for the treatment of chronic hepatitis C:

-  simeprevir,11 in combination with other medicines in the treatment of chronic hepatitis C (CHC)
due to genotype 1 or 4 virus, in adult patients with an advanced stage disease (with F3/F4 liver
fibrosis or presenting with extrahepatic manifestations of HCV) and for whom there are no
other appropriate treatments available;

-  daclatasvir12

 in combination with sofosbuvir for the treatment of chronic hepatitis C due

to genotype 1, 2, 3 or 4 virus:
o  for  patients  with  an  advanced  stage  disease  (with  F3/F4  liver fibrosis  or  presenting
with  extrahepatic  manifestations  of  HCV)  and  for  whom  there  are  no  other
appropriate treatments available,

o  or patients on the waiting list for a liver or kidney transplant,
o  or patients who have undergone a liver transplant and are presenting with recurrence

of hepatitis C infection.

In USA:

Other information:
SOVALDI is currently included in American guidelines (available on line:
http://www.hcvguidelines.org/):

-  sofosbuvir is recommended as a 1st line treatment, in combination with ribavirin (RBV),
with or without peginterferon (PEG-INF), for all patients, all genotypes, for patients
only infected with HCV or co-infected with HIV-HCV,
for  genotype  1  patients  who  have  received  previous  treatment,  sofosbuvir  is
recommended in combination with simeprevir (available in USA, but in the process of
being assessed in Europe), based on the results of a phase 2 study (unpublished).

08.1  Efficacy

8.1.1  Efficacy in patients infected with genotype 1 to 6 HCV

(cid:1)  Characteristics of the different studies

The efficacy of sofosbuvir was evaluated in five phase III clinical studies, on patients infected with
genotype 1 to 6 hepatitis C virus:

-  one  study  was  carried  out  on  treatment-naïve  patients,  infected  with  genotype  1,  4,  5  or  6
hepatitis C virus, in combination with peginterferon alpha 2a and ribavirin (NEUTRINO study),
four studies were conducted on patients infected with genotype 2 or 3 HCV, in combination
with ribavirin, of which:

-

o  one was on treatment-naïve patients (FISSION study),
o  one  was  on  patients  intolerant  to  or  not  eligible  for  interferon  or  refusing  interferon

(POSITRON study),

o  one was on patients who had already been treated with interferon (FUSION study),
o  one was on all types of patient, independent of their treatment history or their ability to

receive interferon-based treatment (VALENCE study).

In  these  studies,  patients  had  a  compensated  liver  disease,  in  particular  compensated  cirrhosis.
Sofosbuvir  was  administered  at  a  dose  of  400  mg,  once  daily.  The  ribavirin  dose  was  calculated
based on weight, and was 1000-1200 mg/day in two divided doses, and, if required, the peginterferon
alpha  2a  dose  was  180  µg  per  week.  The  treatment  duration  was  set  in  each  study  and  was  not
guided by the HCV serum RNA levels of patients (no response guided algorithm).
During the clinical studies, HCV serum RNA levels were measured using the COBAS TaqMan HCV
test  (version  2.0),  used  with  the  High  Pure  system.  Analysis  results  had  a  lower  quantification  limit
(LQL) of 25 IU/ml.

The primary efficacy endpoint in all studies was sustained virological response (SVR), defined by an
HCV RNA level below the LQL, 12 weeks after the end of treatment (SVR12).13

13 Agreement between SVR12 and SVR24 (SVR at 24 weeks after the end of treatment), after treatment with sofosbuvir in
combination with ribavirin or ribavirin plus pegylated interferon, demonstrates a positive indicative value of 99% and a
negative indicative value of 99%.

(cid:1)  Results

Clinical studies on patients with genotype 1, 4, 5 or 6 chronic hepatitis C virus

Treatment-naïve patients - NEUTRINO (study 110)
NEUTRINO  was  a  non-comparative  study,  evaluating  a  12  week  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  in
combination  with  peginterferon  alpha  2a  +  ribavirin  (SOF  +  PEG+  RBV)  in  patients  infected  with
genotype 1, 4, 5 or 6 HCV and who were treatment-naive.

The median age of patients treated (n = 327) was 54 years (19-70 years); 64% were male; 79% were
white,  17%  black  and  14%  Hispanic;  the  mean  body  mass  index  was  29  kg/m2  (18-56  kg/m2);  78%
had  an  HCV  RNA  level  of  >  6  log10  IU/ml  at  inclusion;  17%  had  cirrhosis;  89%  were  infected  with
genotype  1  HCV  (genotype  1a:  69%;  genotype  1b:  20%;  genotype  1a/1b:  0.3%)  and  11%  with  a
genotype  4,  5  or  6  virus;  71%  did  not  have  the  C/C  allele  of  the  IL28B  gene  (factor  indicative  of
response).

Of  the  327  patients  included  and  treated,  only  seven  discontinued  treatment  prematurely  (including
five due to the occurrence of an adverse event).
The sustained virological response rate (SVR12) was 90.5% (95% CI [86.6 – 93.5]).

The SVR12 rates were high not only for patients with the C/C IL28B allele but also non-C/C (C/T or
T/T) patients. Despite there only being a small number of genotype 4, 5 or 6 virus patients included in
this study (n = 35), results suggested a high efficacy for these less common genotypes.
The  number  of  cirrhotic  patients  was  also  low,  but  results  suggest  high  efficacy,  close  to  that  for
non-cirrhotic patients.
Virological failure was not reported during treatment (failures were all post-treatment relapses) and no
resistance to sofosbuvir was observed during this study.

Clinical studies on patients with chronic genotype 2 or 3 hepatitis C virus

Treatment-naïve adults –FISSION study (study 1231)
FISSION was an open-label, randomised, controlled study, with the primary aim being to demonstrate
the  non-inferiority  of  12  weeks  of  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  plus  ribavirin  (SOF + RBV)  versus
24 weeks of treatment with peginterferon alpha 2a plus ribavirin (PEG-INF + RBV) in patients infected
with genotype 2 or 3 HCV, and who were treatment-naïve. Non-inferiority was established if the lower
two  groups
limit  of
(sofosbuvir/ribavirin - peginterferon alpha 2a/ribavirin)  was greater than -15%. A superiority analysis
was scheduled if non-inferiority was demonstrated.

in  response  between

the  confidence

the  difference

interval

the

for

The  dose  of  ribavirin  used  in  the  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  group  was  calculated  based  on  weight
(1000-1200  mg/day),  while  in  the  peginterferon  alpha  2a  +  ribavirin  group  it  was  800  mg/day
regardless  of  weight.  Patients  were  randomised  into  two  groups  (1:1),  with  stratification  based  on
cirrhosis status (presence versus absence), HCV genotype (2 versus 3) and HCV RNA on inclusion
(< 6 log10 IU/ml versus ≥ 6 log10 IU/ml). Patients with genotype 2 or 3 HVC were recruited at a ratio of
approximately 1 to 3.

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

14/37

A total of 527 patients were randomised into this study (263 in the SOF + RBV group versus 264 in
the PEG-INF + RBV group); 499 received at least one dose of treatment (256 versus 243) and 477
were included in the PP (per protocol) population analysis (246 versus 231).
The  median  age  of  patients  treated  (n  =  499)  was  50  years  (range:  19-77  years);  66%  of  patients
were male; 87% were white, 3% black and 14% Hispanic; the mean body mass index was 28 kg/m2
(17-52  kg/m2);  57%  had  an  HCV  RNA  level  of  >  6  log10  IU/ml  at  inclusion;  20%  had  compensated
cirrhosis; 72% were infected with genotype 3 HCV.

In the PP analysis, the sustained virological response at 12 weeks (SVR12) was 69.1% (170/246) in
the sofosbuvir + ribavirin group versus 70.1% (162/231) in the peginterferon alpha + ribavirin group.
The difference in SVR12 rates between the sofosbuvir + ribavirin and peginterferon alpha + ribavirin
groups was -1% (95% CI [-8.7%; 6.7%]), which corresponds to the predefined non-inferiority criterion
(lower limit for the 95% CI of the difference >-15%). This result was confirmed in the ITT (intention to
treat) analysis, with an SVR12 of 66.8% (95% CI [60.7%; 72.5%]) in the sofosbuvir + ribavirin group
versus  66.7%  (95%  CI  [60.4%;  72.6%])  in  the  peginterferon  alpha  +  ribavirin  group  (difference  of
0.3% [-7.5%; 8.0%]). The ITT analysis does not demonstrate superiority of sofosbuvir + ribavirin dual
therapy over 12 weeks compared with peginterferon alpha + ribavirin over 24 weeks (Table 5).

Note:  this  study  was  initially  carried  out  with  patients  with  genotype  2  or  3  HCV  as  a  comparable
response  was  expected  for  these  two  genotypes.  However,  due  to  a  lower  response  for  genotype
3 compared  with  genotype  2,  sofosbuvir  was  subsequently  developed  with  a  24  week  treatment
duration  in  combination  with  RBV  (VALENCE  study)  and  12  weeks  in  combination  with  PEG+RBV
dual  therapy  for  genotype  3  patients  (LONESTAR  2  study).  The  treatment  duration  for  genotype
3 patients recommended in the SPC is therefore 24 weeks in combination with RBV or 12 weeks in
combination with PEG+RBV.
Only results from the FISSION study for genotype 2 patients correspond to the recommended dosage
regimen for sofosbuvir.

Adults intolerant to or not eligible for interferon or refusing interferon - POSITRON (study 107)
POSITRON  was  a  controlled,  randomised,  double-blind  study  evaluating  a  12  week  treatment  with
sofosbuvir  plus  ribavirin  (SOF  +  RBV,  n  =  207)  versus  placebo  (n  =  71)  in  patients  who  were
intolerant to (9%) or not eligible for (44%) interferon or refusing interferon (47%).
Patients  were  randomised  according  to  a  3:1  ratio,  with  stratification  based  on  cirrhosis  status
(presence versus absence).

The median age of patients treated (n = 278) was 54 years (range: 21 - 75 years); 54% were male;
91%  were  white,  5%  black  and  11%  Hispanic;  the  mean  body  mass  index  was  28  kg/m2  (range:
18 - 53  kg/m2);  70%  had  an  HCV  RNA  level  of  >  6  log10  IU/ml  on  inclusion;  16%  had  cirrhosis;
49% were  infected  with  genotype  3  hepatitis  C  virus.  The  majority  had  never  received  anti-HCV
treatment previously (81.3%).

Previously treated adults – FUSION study (study 108)
FUSION  was  a  randomised,  double-blind  study  evaluating  a  treatment  of  12  or  16  weeks  with
sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  in  patients  who  had  not  achieved  SVR  with  a  previous  interferon-based
treatment  (non-response  or  relapse).  Patients  were  randomised  according  to  a  1:1  ratio,  with
stratification based on cirrhosis status (presence versus absence) and HCV genotype (2 versus 3).

The median age of patients treated (n = 201) was 56 years (range: 24 - 70 years); 70% were male;
87%  were  white,  3%  black  and  9%  Hispanic;  the  mean  body  mass  index  was  29  kg/m2
(19 - 44 kg/m2);  73%  had  an  HCV  RNA  level  >  6  log10  IU/ml  on  inclusion;  34%  had  cirrhosis;
63% were infected with genotype 3 HCV; 75% had relapsed.

The  virological  response  rate  (SVR12)  was  50%  with  12  weeks  of  treatment  versus  71%  with
16 weeks of treatment (23% difference, 95% CI [11% – 35%]).

Treatment-naïve or previously treated adults - VALENCE study (study 133)
VALENCE  was  a  phase  III  study  that  evaluated  sofosbuvir  in  combination  with  ribavirin  for  the
treatment of genotype 2 or 3 HCV infection in treatment-naïve patients or those who had not achieved
SVR  with  a  previous  interferon-based  treatment,  including  patients  presenting  with  compensated
cirrhosis. The study was designed to compare sofosbuvir plus ribavirin with placebo over 12 weeks.
However,  based  on  data  from  studies  described  above,  blinding  was  lifted  and  all  patients  infected
with genotype 2 HCV continued to receive sofosbuvir plus ribavirin for 12 weeks, while treatment for
patients infected with genotype 3 HCV had their treatment extended to 24 weeks.

Eleven  patients  infected  with  genotype  3  HCV  had  already  received  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  and

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

17/37

ribavirin for 12 weeks at the time of the change.

The median age of patients treated (n = 419) was 51 years (range: 19 - 74 years); 60% were male;
the  median  body  mass  index  was  25  kg/m2 (range:  17 -  44 kg/m2);  the mean  HCV  RNA  level  was
6.4 log10 IU/ml on inclusion; 21% had cirrhosis; 78% were infected with genotype 3 HCV; 65% had
relapsed.

Table  11  presents  the  response  rates  for  the  groups  treated  with  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  for  12 weeks  and
24 weeks.

Patients receiving placebo were not included in the tables as SVR12 was not achieved.

Efficacy in special patient populations

Patients co-infected with HCV/HIV –PHOTON-1 study (study 123)
PHOTON is an open-label clinical study that evaluated the efficacy of 12 or 24 weeks of treatment
with sofosbuvir and ribavirin in patients with chronic genotype 1, 2 or 3 hepatitis C and co-infected
with HIV-1. Patients infected with genotype 2 or  3 HCV  were treatment-naïve or had already been
treated, whereas patients with genotype 1 were naïve to any previous treatments.
Treatment lasted:

-  12 weeks for genotype 2 or 3 treatment-naïve patients,
-  24 weeks for genotype 2 or 3 patients who had received prior treatment,
-  24 weeks for genotype 1 patients.

Patients  received  400  mg  of  sofosbuvir  and  a  dose  of  ribavirin  based  on  their  weight  (1000  mg  for
patients weighing < 75 kg or 1200 mg for those weighing ≥ 75 kg). Patients were either not taking an
antiretroviral treatment and had a CD4+ lymphocyte count of > 500 cells/mm3, or they were taking an

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

18/37

antiretroviral treatment, with an undetectable HIV-1 viral load, and had  a CD4+ lymphocyte count of
> 200 cells/mm3. Ninety five percent of patients were receiving an antiretroviral treatment at the time
of their recruitment.
Preliminary SVR12 data are available for 210 patients.

The  majority  of  patients  had  a  chronic  genotype  3  HCV  virus  infection  (61.3%),  did  not  have  liver
cirrhosis (90.3%) and had an elevated HCV RNA level (≥ 6 log10 IU/ml) (74.2%).

Patients awaiting liver transplant –TRANSPLANT 2 study (P7977-2025)
TRANSPLANT is an open-label clinical study that evaluated the safety and efficacy of sofosbuvir and
ribavirin administered to patients infected with HCV before receiving a liver transplant, to prevent HCV
re-infection after transplantation.
Patients  infected  with  HCV,  regardless  of  the  genotype,  with  hepatocellular  carcinoma  (HCC)
received  400  mg  of  sofosbuvir  and  1000-1200  mg  of  ribavirin  per  day  for  a  maximum  period  of
24 weeks,  a  duration  that  was  subsequently  extended  to  48  weeks,  or  up  to  liver  transplantation,
depending on whichever event happened first.

The  primary  efficacy  endpoint  was  post-transplantation  virological  response  (pTVR,  defined  by  an
HVC RNA level < LQL 12 weeks after transplantation).

An interim analysis was carried out for 61 patients who received sofosbuvir and ribavirin; the majority
of  patients  had  genotype  1  HCV,  44  had  a  CPT  A  score  while  17  had  a  CPT  B  score.14  Of  these
61 patients, 44 received a liver transplant after treatment with sofosbuvir and ribavirin for a duration
ranging from 24 to 48 weeks; 41 had an HCV RNA level < LQL at the time of transplantation.
The virological response rates of the 41 transplant patients, who had an HCV RNA level < LQL, are
found in Table 16. The duration of viral suppression before transplantation was the factor that was the
most indicative of pTVR for patients whose HCV RNA level was < LQL at the time of transplantation.

14 The Child-Turcotte-Pugh (CPT) score is calculated based on five parameters (bilirubinaemia, albuminaemia, prothrombin
ratio, presence or not of ascites or encephalopathy) and indicates the severity of the liver disease. The higher the score, the
more severe the liver disease. Typically, compensated cirrhosis is CPT A (score 5-6 points) and decompensated cirrhosis
has CPT B (7-9 points) or C (10-15 points) scores.

08.2  Safety/Adverse effects

8.2.1  Safety analysis population – pivotal studies

Safety data presented in this dossier come from the analysis of data from 1337 patients included in

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

22/37

safety analyses of phase III studies.
A pooled analysis of data was carried out as a function of treatment regimen and treatment duration.

8.2.2  Main types of adverse events –phase III studies

Regardless  of  the  duration  of  the  treatment  regimen  used,  patients  treated  with  sofosbuvir  had  a
premature treatment discontinuation rate of ≤ 4%, including less than 2% for an adverse event.

In  the  FISSION  study  that  compared  treatment for  12  weeks  with  SOF  +  RBV  versus  treatment  for
24 weeks  with  PEG  +RBV  in  patients  infected  with  genotype  2  or  3  HCV,  the  premature  treatment
discontinuation rate was 4.3% versus 22.2%, including 1.2% versus 10.7% for adverse effects. In the
NEUTRINO  study,  in  which  the  use  of  PEG+RBV  dual  therapy  was  limited  to  12  weeks  in
combination  with  sofosbuvir  for  genotype  1  patients,  the  premature  discontinuation  rate  was  low
(2.4%), which was also the case in the VALENCE study (1.6%) where SOF + RBV dual therapy was
used for 24 weeks.

8.2.3  Adverse  events  causing  premature  treatment  discontinuation  –  phase  III
studies

The  incidence  of  treatment  discontinuation  for  an  adverse  event  was  very  low  in  all  groups
treated with sofosbuvir: 0% in the SOF+RBV 16 weeks group, 1.6% for the SOF+RBV 12 weeks
group and 2.4% for the SOF+PEG+RBV 12 weeks group.

However, this incidence was 11.9% for patients treated with PEG+RBV for 24 weeks.

Types  of  adverse  events  causing  treatment  discontinuation  are  those  typically  associated  with
the use of pegylated interferons with ribavirin, and in particular:

-
-
-

anaemia and other blood disorders (anaemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia),
general disorders (fatigue, pain, nausea),
neuropsychological disorders (depression, insomnia, anxiety, irritability, loss of
consciousness).

Anaemia  is  an  adverse  effect  known  to  be  linked  with  the  use  of  RBV  in  particular,  due  to  its
accumulation in red blood cells leading to their destruction (haemolysis). The risk of anaemia is
increased when it is combined with PEG.
Neutropenia and leucopenia were only reported in the PEG groups.

8.2.6  Summary of safety profile (taken from the SPC)

“During treatment with sofosbuvir in combination with ribavirin or peginterferon alpha and ribavirin, the
most  frequently  reported  adverse  drug  reactions  were  consistent  with  the  expected  safety  profile  of
ribavirin  and  peginterferon  alpha  treatment,  without  increasing  the  frequency  or  severity  of  the
expected adverse drug reactions.

Assessment  of  adverse  reactions  is  based  on  all  data  from  five  phase  III  clinical  studies  (both
controlled and uncontrolled subjects).

The  proportion  of  subjects  who  permanently  discontinued  treatment  due  to  adverse  reactions  was
1.4% for subjects receiving placebo, 0.5% for subjects receiving sofosbuvir + ribavirin for 12 weeks,
0%  for  subjects  receiving  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  for  16  weeks,  11.1%  for  subjects  receiving

Tabulated summary of adverse reactions
SOVALDI has mainly been studied in combination with ribavirin, with or without peginterferon alpha.
In  this  context,  no  adverse  drug  reactions  specific  to  sofosbuvir  have  been  identified.  The  most
common adverse drug reactions occurring in subjects receiving sofosbuvir and ribavirin or sofosbuvir,
ribavirin and peginterferon alpha were fatigue, headache, nausea and insomnia.

The following adverse drug reactions have been identified with sofosbuvir in combination with ribavirin
or in combination with peginterferon alpha and ribavirin (Table 25). The adverse reactions are listed
below  by  system  organ  class  and  frequency.  Adverse  reactions  are  defined  as:  very  common
(≥ 1/10),  common  (≥  1/100,  <  1/10),  uncommon  (≥  1/1000,  <  1/100),  rare  (≥  1/10000,  <  1/1000)  or
very rare (< 1/10,000).

08.3  Usage/prescription data

SOVALDI has had a Temporary Authorisation for use by a cohort [Autorisation Temporaire
d’Utilisation (ATU) de cohort] since 30 September 2013.
Extension of the indication of the temporary authorisation for use by a cohort was granted by ANSM
on 06 December 2013.

Temporary authorisation for use [ATU] indications:

Previous indication

New indication

Sofosbuvir, used within the context of a Temporary
Authorisation for use by a cohort, is indicated for the
treatment of adults with chronic HCV infection who:

-  are  on  the  waiting  list  for  a  liver  transplant  and  require

treatment to avoid re-infection with HCV, or

-  have undergone a liver transplant and are presenting with
recurrence of an aggressive infection with the hepatitis C
virus, translating as worsening in liver impairment, with a
life expectancy of less than 12 months in the absence of
treatment.

Sofosbuvir,  used  within  the  context  of  a  Temporary
Authorisation  for  use  by  a  cohort,  is  indicated  for  the
treatment  of  adult  patients  with  chronic  hepatitis  C
infection:

-  presenting with a disease in an advanced stage for which
there are no appropriate treatment alternatives available.

Or

-  who are on the waiting list for a liver transplant

Or

- have undergone a liver transplant and are presenting with
relapse of an aggressive infection of the hepatitis C virus.

08.4  Summary & discussion

The  efficacy  of  sofosbuvir  was  evaluated  in  five  phase  III  clinical  studies,  in  patients  infected  with
genotype 1 to 6 hepatitis C virus and who have a compensated liver disease, in particular cirrhosis.
Sofosbuvir  was  administered  at  a  dose  of  400  mg,  once  daily.  The  ribavirin  dose  was  calculated
based on weight, and was 1000-1200 mg/day in two divided doses, and, if required, the peginterferon
alpha  2a  dose  was  180  µg  per  week.  The  treatment  duration  was  set  in  each  study  and  was  not
guided by the HCV serum RNA levels of patients (no response-guided algorithm).

The primary efficacy endpoint in all studies was sustained virological response (SVR), defined by an
HCV  RNA  level  below  the  lower  quantification  limit  (LQL)15
  12  weeks  after  the  end  of  treatment
(SVR12).

Efficacy for genotype 1, 4, 5 and 6 patients:
A non-comparative phase III study (NEUTRINO study), evaluated the efficacy of a 12 week treatment
with  sofosbuvir  in  combination  with  peginterferon  alpha  2a  +  ribavirin  in  327  patients  infected  with
genotype 1, 4, 5 or 6 HCV, who were treatment-naive. The majority of patients included (89%) were
infected  with  genotype  1  HCV  (genotype  1a:  69%;  genotype  1b:  20%;  genotype  1a/1b:  0.3%)  and
11% by genotype 4, 5 or 6 HCV; 17% had cirrhosis.
Of  the  327  patients  included  and  treated,  seven  discontinued  their  treatment  prematurely  (including
five due to the occurrence of an adverse event).
In  this  study,  the  virological  response  rate  with  sofosbuvir  +  peginterferon  alpha  2a  +  ribavirin  triple
therapy  for  12  weeks  (recommended  duration  in  the  Marketing  Authorisation)  was  90.5%  in  the
overall  population  (including  all  genotypes),  and  approximately  80%  for  cirrhotic  patients.  This
response  was  90% (262/292) for genotype  1  patients  (genotype  1a:  92%; genotype  1b:  83%).  Very
few  patients  (n  =  35)  with  genotype  4,  5  or  6  were  included  in  this  study.  A  higher  response  was,
however, noted for these less common genotypes: 27/28 for patients infected with genotype 4 HCV;
1/1 patient infected with genotype 5 HCV and 6/6 patients infected with genotype 6 HCV.
Furthermore,  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  dual  therapy  for  24  weeks  (duration  recommended  in  the
Marketing  Authorisation  for  patients  not  eligible  for  or  intolerant  to  peginterferon  alpha)  is  effective
with an SVR12 rate ranging from 50% to 76% depending on the study (QUANTUM or PHOTON-1).

Sofosbuvir  was  not  studied  in  patients  with  genotype  1,  4,  5  and  6  HCV  infection  who  had  been
treated previously.

Although there is a risk of overestimation of the level of effect, given the fact that these data are from
rates  observed  with
a  non-comparative  clinical  study
sofosbuvir + peginterferon + ribavirin triple therapy with a short-term treatment length of 12 weeks in
the  NEUTRINO  study  are  higher  than  those  observed  in  clinical  studies  that  evaluated  treatments
currently
(in  particular
peginterferon + ribavirin  +  protease  inhibitor  [boceprevir  or  telaprevir]  triple  therapies  with  longer
treatment durations, ranging from 24 to 48 weeks).

Efficacy for genotype 2 and 3 patients:
In a controlled phase III study (FISSION study), 12 weeks treatment with sofosbuvir plus ribavirin was
non-inferior  to  24  weeks  treatment  with  peginterferon  alpha  2a  plus  ribavirin  dual  therapy  (current
standard use) for patients infected with genotype 2 or 3 HCV and treatment-naive, with an SVR12 of
66.8%  (95%  CI  [60.7%;  72.5%])  in  the  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  group  versus  66.7%  (95%  CI  [60.4%;
72.6%]) in the peginterferon alpha + ribavirin group (difference of 0.3% [-7.5%; 8.0%]).
A  subgroup  analysis  according to  viral  genotype  showed  a  difference  in  response  between  patients
infected with genotype 2 HCV and those infected with genotype 3:
•
12 weeks versus 78% (52/67) with PEG+RBV over 24 weeks.

for  patients  with  genotype  2  HCV,  the  SVR12  rate  was  97%  (68/70)  with  SOF+RBV  over

used with the High Pure system. Analysis results had a lower quantification limit (LQL) of 25 IU/ml.

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

28/37

for  patients  with  genotype  3  HCV,  the  SVR12  rate  was  56%  (102/183)  with  SOF+RBV  over

•
12 weeks versus 63% (110/176) with PEG+RBV over 24 weeks.
In  a  controlled  phase  III  study  (POSITRON  study),  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  over  12  weeks  was
compared with placebo for patients infected with genotype 2 or 3 HCV, intolerant to, not eligible for or
refusing  interferon.  In  this  study,  the  SVR12  rate  was  78%  (versus  0%  in  the  placebo  group).  This
response was higher for patients with genotype 2 HCV (93%) than for those infected with genotype
3 HCV (61%).
A  controlled  phase  III  study  (FUSION  study)  compared  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  dual  therapy  over
12 weeks  versus  16  weeks  in  patients  previously  treated  with  peginterferon  +  ribavirin  dual  theapy
(essentially those patients who had relapsed). In this study, the SVR12 rate was 50% for 12 weeks of
treatment versus 71% for the 16 week course. This response was higher for patients with genotype
2 HCV than those infected with genotype 3 HCV, both for the 12 week treatment (82% versus 30%)
and the 16 week duration (89% versus 62%).
Due to the lower response for patients infected with genotype 3 virus, the 12 week treatment regimen
has only been validated by the Marketing Authorisation for patients infected with genotype 2 virus. For
genotype 3, sofosbuvir has subsequently been  developed  with a treatment duration of 24 weeks as
dual  therapy  with  ribavirin  (VALENCE  study)  and  12  weeks  as  triple  therapy  with  peginterferon  and
ribavirin  (LONESTAR  2  study),  corresponding  to  the  regimens  recommended  in  the  Marketing
Authorisation.

In conclusion,

•

•

for genotype  2  patients, a  12  week  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  (without  the  use  of
interferon) has been effective, with SVR12 rates of 97% for treatment-naïve patients (versus
78%  with  24  weeks  of  treatment  with  peginterferon  alpha  2a  plus  ribavirin  dual  therapy)
(FISSION  study)  and  93%  for  patients  not  eligible  for,  intolerant  to  or  refusing  interferon
(POSITRON  study).  For  patients  who  had  already  received  interferon-based  treatment
(FUSION study), the  SVR12  rate  was  82%  with  a  12  week  treatment  course  and  89%  with
16 weeks of treatment.
for  genotype  3  patients,  treatment  over  24  weeks  with  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  (VALENCE
study) has been effective, with SVR12 rates of 93% for treatment-naïve patients and 77% for
previously  treated  patients.  Treatment  for  12  weeks  with  sofosbuvir + peginterferon + RBV
triple therapy (LONESTAR-2: phase II study) also appears to be effective (SVR12: 20/24).

Efficacy in special populations:
The efficacy of sofosbuvir was also shown in certain special patient populations:

•

•

for  patients  co-infected  with  HCV-HIV,  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  +  ribavirin  dual  therapy
(PHOTON-1 study) was effective, with SVR12 rates of between 75% and 93% depending on
the viral genotype. The results from this study suggest a similar response between patients
co-infected with HIV/HCV and those infected with just HCV.
for  patients  awaiting  a
treatment  with
liver
sofosbuvir + ribavirin was shown to be effective in preventing re-infection of the transplanted
organ with HCV, with a post-transplantation virological response of 62% (23/37) for patients
presenting  with  an  HCV  RNA  level  <  LQL  at  the  time  of  transplantation  (results  from  an
interim analysis).

(TRANSPLANT  2  study),

transplant

No cases of resistance to treatment have been observed for patients treated with sofosbuvir in any of
the phase III studies.

It should be noted that there are limited data for cirrhotic patients, as well as for patients who did not
respond  to  a  previous  interferon-based  treatment  (“null  responders”),  in  particular  for  previously
treated genotype 1 patients, who were not included in these studies.
The SPC states that an extension of the treatment duration beyond 12 weeks, and up to 24 weeks,
should be considered in particular for subgroups of patients presenting with one or more factors that
are  already  associated  with  low  response  rates  to  interferon-based  treatments  (e.g.  advanced
fibrosis/cirrhosis, elevated initial viral loads, being of African origin, IL28B non CC genotype and “null
responders” to a previous peginterferon alpha and ribavirin-based treatment).

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

29/37

Safety
The safety profile of sofosbuvir was satisfactory in the clinical studies. Sofosbuvir was mainly studied
in combination with ribavirin, with or without peginterferon alpha. In this context, it is difficult to identify
the adverse effects that are specific to sofosbuvir. The most commonly reported adverse effects were
fatigue,  headaches,  nausea  and  insomnia.  These  effects  are  those  corresponding  to  the  expected
safety  profile  for  treatment  with  ribavirin  and  peginterferon  alpha,  without  an  increase  in  their
frequency or their severity. The proportion of patients who permanently discontinued treatment due to
adverse effects was 1.4% for patients who received placebo, 0.5% for those who received sofosbuvir
+  ribavirin  for  12  weeks,  0%  for  patients  receiving  sofosbuvir + ribavirin  for  16  weeks,  and  2.4% for
patients  who  received  sofosbuvir  +  peginterferon  alpha  +  ribavirin  over  12  weeks  versus  11.1%  for
those who received peginterferon alpha + ribavirin over 24 weeks.

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

30/37

09  THERAPEUTIC USE

French guidelines for the treatment of hepatitis C are in the process of being updated by the AFEF
[French Association for Liver Studies] and ANRS [French National Agency for AIDS research], as the
result of changes in the treatment of this infection due to the arrival of new treatments.16

Recommended  treatments  in  the  management  of  HCV  before  the  Marketing  Authorisation  of
sofosbuvir are summarised below.

Populations  Recommendations
Naive:
PEG-IFN, RBV ± boceprevir or telaprevir over 24 to 48 weeks
After failure of previous treatment:
PEG-IFN, RBV + boceprevir or telaprevir over 48 weeks

Genotype 1

Genotypes 2
and 3

Genotype 4

Genotypes 5
and 6

Co-infected
with HIV

d cirrhosis

transplant

Naive:
PEG-IFN + RBV over 24 weeks

After failure of previous treatment:
no approved regimen
Naive:
PEG-IFN + RBV over 48 weeks
After failure of previous treatment:
no approved regimen
Naive:
PEG-IFN + RBV over 48 weeks
After failure of previous treatment:
no approved regimen

GT1:
PEG-IFN, RBV and boceprevir or telaprevir

Other GT:
PEG-IFN + RBV over 48 weeks regardless of the genotype

Treatment  highly  recommended,  in  the  absence  of  contraindications,  for  patients  with
compensated  cirrhosis,  in  order  to  avoid  chronic  HCV  infection  complications  that  occur
exclusively in this group in the short- to medium-term
Moreover, sustained virological response (SVR) with PEG-IFN + RBV is lower for patients
with  advanced  fibrosis  or  cirrhosis  than  those  patients  with  moderate  fibrosis.  Although
superior  to  dual  therapy,  SVR  rates  with  triple  therapy  for  genotype  1  patients  are  also
negatively affected by the stage of fibrosis.
Relapse of HCV infection is more or less similar after liver transplant following cirrhosis or
HCC linked to HCV. An elevated viral load, the occurrence of symptomatic hepatitis or high
immunosuppression  are  factors  for  unfavourable  progress,  justifying  the  inclusion  of
patients in treatment protocols.
Antiviral  treatment  for  patients  awaiting  a  transplant  protects  against  re-infection  of  the
transplanted organ if an SVR is achieved.
However, more than half of patients have contraindications for the use of PEG-IFN + RBV,
and  the  response  to  treatment  is  generally  very  low  in  this  group  of  patients  with  a  very
advanced  liver  condition.  Antiviral  treatment  is  indicated  for  patients  with  preserved  liver
function  (Child-Pugh  A)  for  whom  a  transplant  is  indicated  for  HCC.  For  patients  with
Child-Pugh B cirrhosis, antiviral treatment may be offered on an individual basis in specialist
centres,  preferentially  for  patients  with  factors  indicative  of  a  good  response,  such  as
patients infected with genotype 2 or 3 HCV, or patients with a low viral load. Patients with
Child-Pugh C cirrhosis should not be treated with IFN-based regimens, due to the increased
risk of potentially fatal complications.
For people with a severe liver condition that can be treated before transplantation, antiviral
medication should be started as early as possible, with the aim of achieving an SVR, or at
least a decrease in HCV RNA at the time of the transplant.
Haematological  adverse  events  (anaemia,  neutropenia,  thrombocytopenia,  etc.)  are

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

33/37

particularly common for patients with end stage liver disease due to portal hypertension.
There  are  no  data  on  the  use  of  protease  inhibitor-  (PI)  based  triple  therapies  (BOC  and
TVR) in the treatment of patients on the waiting list for transplant, with a very advanced liver
disease.  The two  PIs  (TVR  and  BOC)  present a  haematological  toxicity  and  an increased
risk of serious infections, of the type that the adverse effects profile in this group of patients
may be particularly difficult.

While  awaiting  the  French  guidelines  for  the  treatment  of  patients  infected  with  hepatitis  C  virus
anticipated  in  2015,  The  French  Association  for  Liver  Studies  (AFEF)  has  put  forward  expert
opinions17
  regarding  treatment  choices  for  hepatitis  C.  AFEF  no  longer  recommends  the  use  of
VICTRELIS (boceprevir) and INCIVO (telaprevir) in the treatment of chronic hepatitis C with genotype
1 HCV.

According to the DHUMEAUX report,18 it is recommended to propose treatment with sofosbuvir as a
priority  for  all  patients  with  a  fibrosis  score  ≥  F2,  as  well  as  for  certain  special  populations,
independent  of  the  degree  of  fibrosis:  “patients  with  extrahepatic  manifestations  of  the  hepatitis  C
virus,  patients  awaiting  an  organ  transplant,  women  wishing  to  become  pregnant,  drug  users  and
those in prison.”

Role of SOVALDI in the therapeutic strategy
Given  the  levels  of  virological  efficacy  irrespective  of  HCV  genotype  or  the  severity  of  hepatic
impairment  and  its  satisfactory  safety  profile,  sofosbuvir,  in  combination  with  other  antivirals,
represents the new standard treatment for patients with chronic hepatitis C.

010

TRANSPARENCY COMMITTEE CONCLUSIONS

In  view  of  all  the  above  information,  and  following  the  debate  and  vote,  the  Committee’s
opinion is as follows:

010.1 Actual Benefit

(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)  The  severity  of  hepatitis  C  is  linked  to  it  commonly  becoming  a  chronic  condition,  which  in  the
long-term  can  lead  to  cirrhosis,  hepatocellular  impairment  or  hepatocellular  carcinoma.  Of  the
six genotypes of the hepatitis C virus, genotype 1 is predominant.
(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) This medicinal product is a first-line therapy in combination with other antiviral agents.
(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) This medicinal product is intended as curative therapy.
(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) The efficacy/adverse effects ratio is high.
(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) There are treatment alternatives.

(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) Public health benefit:
The  public  health  burden  of  chronic  hepatitis  C  in  adults  is  moderate  (for  2013,  WHO  has
estimated  157,000  DALYs  for  Europe,  which  is  0.05%  of  the  total  number  of  DALYs  for  this
region).
Reduction in morbidity and mortality attributed to chronic hepatitis C is a continuing public health
need, included in the third national plan against hepatitis B and C 2009-2012.
Clinical  study  data  have  shown  a  significant  impact  for  treatment  with  sofosbuvir  on  sustained
virological  response  rates  for  non-cirrhotic  patients,  for  all  genotypes,  and  also  for  patients

17 Expert opinion of AFEF on the treatment of hepatitis C in France, March 2014. Available at: http://www.afef.asso.fr/
(consulted on 21 March 2014)
18 Report on the treatment of people infected with the hepatitis B or hepatitis C virus. Consulted on 5 May 2014 (publication
scheduled for 19 May 2014).

HAS - Medical, Economic and Public Health Assessment Division
Opinion 1

34/37

co-infected  with  HIV-1.  This  impact  is  less  for  cirrhotic  patients.  Modelling  results  suggest  that
sofosbuvir  has  an  additional  impact,  compared  with  protease  inhibitors,  on  the  morbidity  and
mortality of patients treated (progress towards chronic disease, liver fibrosis, liver cancer, death,
horizon  2030).  Moreover,  in  the  absence  of  complete  study  design  elements  describing  this
modelling,  these  results  are  to  be  considered  with  extreme  caution.  However,  generally,  for
patients  presenting  with  evolved  fibrosis,  there  continues  to  be  uncertainty  regarding  progress
towards the process of carcinogenesis.

The expected impact on quality of life of patients treated, as the result of improved response rate
and a shorter treatment time leading to fewer adverse effects, can not be quantified.
There  is  expected  to  be  an  impact  on  both  the  organisation  of  care  as  well  as  the  healthcare
system.
Treatment with sofosbuvir, in a combination, appears to provide an additional, partial response to
an identified public health need.
Consequently,  in  the  current  state  of  knowledge,  it  is  expected  that  sofosbuvir  will  offer  a
substantial benefit to public health and on the organisation of care in this indication.

Consequently,  the  Committee  considers  that  the  actual  benefit  of  SOVALDI  is  substantial  in
the Marketing Authorisation indication.

The Committee recommends inclusion on the list of medicines refundable by National Health
Insurance and on the list of medicines approved for hospital use in the indication and at the
dosages in the Marketing Authorisation.

(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1)(cid:1) Proposed reimbursement rate: 65%

010.2 Improvement in Actual Benefit (IAB)

Given:
-

its  higher  efficacy  for  the  most  difficult  to  treat  patients,  in  particular  genotype
1, 4, 5 and  6  patients,  cirrhotic  patients,  pre-transplantation  patients  and  patients
co-infected with HIV and HCV,
its  efficacy  for  genotype  2  and  3  patients,  including  those  with  a  failed  previous
treatment, but with a higher response for genotype 2 (a short-term 12 week treatment of
dual  therapy  with  ribavirin,  without  interferon)  than  for  genotype  3  patients  (requiring
extension of treatment to 24 weeks with dual therapy with ribavirin, or even short-term
12 week treatment as triple therapy with interferon)
its  satisfactory  safety,  resistance  and  interaction  with  other  medicines  profiles
observed during clinical studies,

-

-

the  Committee  considers  that  SOVALDI,  in  combination  with  pegylated  interferon  alpha
and/or ribavirin, provides:

-  a substantial improvement in actual benefit (IAB II) in the treatment of all adult patients

infected with HCV, except for antiviral treatment-naïve genotype 3 patients,

-  a  moderate  improvement  in  actual  benefit  (IAB  III)  in  the  treatment  of  antiviral

treatment-naïve adult patients infected with genotype 3 HCV.

010.3 Target population

Sofosbuvir  is  indicated  in  the  treatment  of  chronic  hepatitis  C  virus  in  adult  patients.  Available
epidemiological data regarding hepatitis C are mainly from the prevalence survey carried out by the
Health Monitoring Institute (InVS) in 2004.19

As  an  indication,  according  to  epidemiological  modelling  (DHUMEAUX  report,  2014),  approximately
200,000 people had chronic HCV at the end of 2013, including 43% at a F0-F1 stage of fibrosis, 49%
at stage F2-F4 and 8% at a stage with complications (decompensated positive cirrhosis and/or HCC).
It  has  also  been  estimated  that  if  64%  of  patients  with  positive  HCV  antibodies  had  so  far  been
detected, less than 50% of patients who are RNA HCV positive had been, given the people cured as
the  result  of  treatment.  Of  the  95,000  people  detected  who  are  RNA  HCV  positive,  approximately
55,000 would never been treated (including 34,000 who in theory would be candidates for treatment)
and approximately 40,000 would have had a failed treatment (including 29,000 who in theory would
be candidates for re-treatment).
In  addition,  through  extrapolation  based  on  this  approach,  as  an  indication  it  can  be  estimated  that
approximately 70,000 would be, in theory, candidates for hepatitis C treatment.

011

TRANSPARENCY COMMITTEE RECOMMENDATIONS

(cid:1) Packaging
The  proposed  packaging  in  vials  of  28  film-coated  tablets  containing  400  mg  of  sofosbuvir  is
appropriate for the prescribing conditions according to the dosage and the treatment duration given:

-

-

the daily recommended dose of sofosbuvir, which corresponds to the use of 1 tablet

  per day,

the recommended treatment duration with sofosbuvir, which is 12 weeks (which is

  three times 28 tablets) or 24 weeks (which is six times 28 tablets).

(cid:1) Other recommendations
SOVALDI  is  the  first  new  substance,  which  when  combined  with  other  antivirals,  whether  they  are
specific  or  not,  could  be  able  to  cure  the  majority  of  patients  with  hepatitis  C,  with  or  without
extrahepatic  manifestations.  Treatment  strategies  are  being  completely  renewed  and  new
substances,  which  may  be  prescribed  in  combination  with  sofosbuvir  or  other  substances,  are
expected in the coming months.

The Committee would therefore like to re-assess this medicinal product in the short-term, depending
on the developments in the clinical data and the scope of the treatment of chronic hepatitis C.

According to the DHUMEAUX report, it is already desirable to propose treatment with sofosbuvir as a

priority for all patients whose liver condition is at a F3 or F4 stage of fibrosis. Patients at the F2 stage
of fibrosis should also be given the new treatments as soon as possible. For F0 and F1 patients,
based on disease progression, which is often very slow over several years allowing the physician to
suspend the prescription while waiting for clarification of therapeutic use, the treatment may be
deferred.

It  is  also  recommended  to  treat  certain  special  populations,  independent  of  the  degree  of  fibrosis,
such  as  patients  waiting  for  an  organ  transplant,  women  wishing  to  become  pregnant,  drug  users,
patients co-infected with HIV and those who are in prison, as well as patients who are presenting with
extrahepatic manifestations of the hepatitis C virus.

Extension of the treatment to the entire population infected with HCV can only be proposed through
an overall public health approach.

(cid:1) Specific requests inherent to reimbursement
The Committee recommends that this medicinal product is given exception drug status.
